      PURPOSE OF TESTIMONY 
16 
Q. 
What is the purpose of the Panel’s testimony in this 
17 
proceeding? 
18 
A. 
The purpose of our testimony is to demonstrate that 
19 
the costs of the Company’s benefits and compensation 
20 
plans are reasonable business expenses that should be 
21 
recovered in rates.  The Panel’s testimony 
22 


--- Page 9 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF 
COMPENSATION/BENEFITS PANEL 
 
-9- 
demonstrates that the Company provides market-
1 
competitive benefits and compensation packages 
2 
designed to attract and retain those employees the 
3 
Company requires to provide customers with safe and 
4 
reliable service.  The Company continues to manage 
5 
proactively long-range costs like those related to 
6 
pensions and health care.     
7 
 
This direct testimony examines the overall level of 
8 
employee “Benefits” and “Compensation” reflected in 
9 
the revenue requirements of this filing and 
10 
demonstrates that the Company’s level of benefits and 
11 
compensation in aggregate is market-competitive and 
12 
meets the Commission’s standards for assessing the 
13 
overall competitiveness and reasonableness of such 
14 
expenditures.  The costs of the Company’s benefits and 
15 
compensation plans constitute reasonable business 
16 
expenses that should be recoverable in rates for the 
17 
reasons discussed below.   
18 
Q. 
What are the elements of the Benefits package that are 
19 
reflected in the revenue requirements of this filing? 
20 
A.  Benefits include retirement, active and retiree 
21 
health, vacation, life insurance, and disability 
22 


--- Page 10 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF 
COMPENSATION/BENEFITS PANEL 
 
-10- 
benefits.   
1 
Q.  What are the elements of Compensation that are 
2 
reflected in the revenue requirements of this filing? 
3 
A. 
Compensation includes base salary, the variable 
4 
component of management pay (also known as the “Annual 
5 
Team Incentive Program” or “ATIP”), and long-term 
6 
equity grants.  
7 
Q. 
Has the Commission articulated criteria to determine 
8 
whether the costs associated with a utility’s benefits 
9 
and compensation plans should be recoverable in rates? 
10 
A. 
Yes.  In the Commission’s rate order, issued February 
11 
21, 2014 in the Con Edison rate cases filed in 2013 
12 
(Case 13-E-0030, 13-G-0031, 13-S-0032)(“2013 Con 
13 
Edison Rate Cases”), the Commission indicated that a 
14 
utility should demonstrate the overall competitiveness 
15 
and reasonableness of its total benefits and 
16 
compensation package by including a comparison with a 
17 
peer group comprised of similarly situated companies, 
18 
including both utilities and general industry.  In its 
19 
rate order issued June 26, 2014 in the United Water 
20 
New York, Inc. rate case (Case 13-W-0295), the 
21 
Commission reaffirmed that to obtain recovery of 
22 


--- Page 11 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF 
COMPENSATION/BENEFITS PANEL 
 
-11- 
variable pay, a utility must demonstrate that the 
1 
overall compensation, including the variable pay 
2 
component, is reasonable relative to similarly 
3 
situated companies. 
4 
Q. 
Has the Commission addressed any other criteria with 
5 
respect to evaluating recovery of costs associated 
6 
with a utility’s benefits and compensation package? 
7 
A. 
Yes. In its rate order in the 2013 Con Edison Rate 
8 
Cases, the Commission noted with approval Con Edison’s 
9 
willingness to conduct its comparative 
10 
compensation/benefits study to achieve at least a 50 
11 
percent matching of positions in a blended peer group 
12 
of utilities and New York metropolitan employers.   
13 
Q. 
What will the Panel address?   
14 
A.  The Panel will address: (1) a review that the Company 
15 
conducted, with the assistance of Aon, of O&R’s total 
16 
benefits and compensation package (“Review”) in 2020 
17 
for non-officer management employees; (2) the 
18 
Company’s compensation and benefits plans for non-
19 
officer management employees, (3) officer and O&R 
20 
Board of Directors (“O&R Board”) compensation; (4) the 
21 
Company’s current labor contract (“Labor Contract”) 
22

-12- 
with the International Brotherhood of Electrical

Workers, Local 503 (“Local 503”); and (5) employee

benefits costs.

Q. 
What was the purpose of the Review? 
4 
A. 
The purpose of the Review was to assess the market 
5 
competitiveness of the Company’s total benefits and 
6 
compensation package for O&R’s non-officer management 
7 
employees.  The Panel describes below the Review 
8

9 
Q. 
In conducting the Review, did the Company re-evaluate 
10 
its benefits and compensation package as compared to 
11 
those offered by similarly situated companies? 
12 
A. 
Yes.  Consistent with Commission policy and typical 
13 
market practice, in assessing the overall 
14 
competitiveness and reasonableness of O&R’s benefits 
15 
and compensation package, the Review compared the 
16 
Company’s package to those offered by a peer group of 
17 
similarly situated companies. 
18 
Q. 
Were the peer companies limited to utility companies? 
19 
A.  No, as recommended by the Commission, the Company 
20 
evaluated total benefits and compensation relative to 
21 
a blended peer group including both utility and non-
22

-13-

companies (“Blended Peer Group”).

3

4 
A. 
As explained below, the Review found that the 
5 
Company’s combined benefits and compensation package 
6 
value for the non-officer management population, are 
7 
within the +/- ten percent range that is considered 
8

Q. 
Does the Panel address the impact COVID-19 has had on 
7 
historical costs? 
8 
A. 
Yes. The impact to the historical year was immaterial 
9 
as half of the employee population is enrolled in 
10 
fully insured plans.  
11 
Q. 
Why does a fully insured plan reduce the impact of 
12 
COVID-19 for the historical year costs? 
13 
A. 
Local 503 employees are covered under a fully insured 
14 
health plan. This means the Company pays contracted 
15 
monthly premiums based on enrollment counts regardless 
16 
of the services used by the employees. As a result, 
17 
there were no savings or additional costs associated 
18 
with this population in the historical year due to 
19 
COVID-19.  
20 
Q. 
Are management employees also in a fully insured plan? 
21 
A. 
No, management employees are in a self-insured plan. 
22 


--- Page 16 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF 
COMPENSATION/BENEFITS PANEL 
 
-16- 
The Company does not pay monthly premiums but is 
1 
responsible for paying all claims. As with the Local 
2 
503 population, the impact to the historical year 
3 
costs due to COVID-19 were immaterial. 
4 
Q. 
Does the Panel expect the impact of COVID-19 to 
5 
continue to be immaterial for the rate period? 
6 
A. 
No, there is much uncertainty of the impact of COVID-
7 
19 going forward. For example, it is unknown whether 
8 
or not significant numbers of employees will seek 
9 
postponed surgeries and doctor visits thus raising 
10 
costs and whether providers will increase premiums to 
11 
recoup any losses from COVID-19.  These are questions 
12 
without reliable precedent upon which answers can be 
13 
forecasted.  Thus, the Company reserves the right to 
14 
seek recovery of these costs later in these 
15 
proceedings as information becomes available. 
16

REVIEW METHODOLOGY 
10 
Q.

11

A. 
The Review compared O&R’s non-officer management 
13

14 
external benchmark data for the following components: 
15 
• Employee benefits (including pre- and post-
16 
retirement); 
17 
• Base salary; 
18 
• Variable pay; and 
19 
• Long-term equity grants. 
20 
Q. 
Please describe the peer companies that were used in 
21 
the Review to analyze the competitiveness and 
22 


--- Page 18 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC.

-18- 
reasonableness of the Company’s non-officer management 
1 
benefit plan designs and annual benefit and 
2

A. 
A peer group of 46 companies (i.e., the Blended Peer 
4 
Group) was used for comparison purposes, including 23 
5

6 
industry peers.

16 
A.

17

18 
experience and skills in the utility industry as 
19 
Orange and Rockland.  The 23 New York metropolitan 
20

22

3 
employees in the New York metropolitan area.  These 
4

5 
Rockland in its non-utility-specific areas such as 
6 
finance, information technology, human resources, and

9

10 
Group also reflects a sample that has available data 
11 
for both compensation and benefit benchmarking based

on survey participation.

Q. 
Is the Blended Peer Group used in the Review (“2020

17 
18-G-0068)(“2017 Blended Peer Group”)? 
18 
A. 
No.  The companies in the 2020 Blended Peer Group and 
19 
the 2018 Blended Peer Group are not identical.  The 
20 
need to substitute new companies into a peer group 
21 
often occurs because not every company continues to 
22 


--- Page 20 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF 
COMPENSATION/BENEFITS PANEL 
 
-20- 
participate in the information surveys that provide 
1 
the data necessary for a benefit and compensation

3

4

8 
Q 
What is included in the employee benefits value 
9 
analysis? 
10 
A. 
There are two components to the benefit analysis.  The 
11 
first component is the employee benefits design 
12 
analysis which compared the design features of the 
13 
benefits programs for new hires at Orange and Rockland 
14 
(e.g., health plan co-payments, deductibles, and co-
15 
insurance) to the design features of the benefits 
16 
programs for new hires at the members of the 2020 
17 
Blended Peer Group. 
18 
 
The second component is the benefit package value 
19 
analysis.  The benefit package value analysis includes 
20 
a pay-weighted assessment of the program features that 
21 
are based on salary (e.g., pension benefit accrual 
22 


--- Page 22 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF 
COMPENSATION/BENEFITS PANEL 
 
-22- 
formulas, thrift savings plan company match 
1 
percentages, and the definition of covered pay).   
2 
Q. 
Please continue. 
3 
A. 
The annual benefit package value at Orange and 
4 
Rockland was measured against the annual benefit 
5 
package value of the peer companies’ benefit designs 
6 
to compare how compensation-based benefit programs 
7 
affect the total value of the benefits packages 
8 
included in the comparison.  If, for example, an 
9 
employee at Company A earns more pay than an employee 
10 
at Company B in the same position, then the value of a 
11 
Thrift Savings Plan company match (i.e., five percent 
12 
of pay) to the employee at Company A will be higher.  
13 
The employee benefit analysis performed in this manner 
14 
allows for a more accurate comparison of the value of 
15 
a benefits package than an analysis that is performed 
16 
on a pay-neutral basis.   
17 
Q. 
Please describe the process used to assess the benefit 
18 
designs of the benefits programs of the Company and 
19 
its peer companies. 
20 
A. 
The benchmarking of employee benefits design was done 
21 
using Aon’s Benefit Index (“Benefit Index”).  The 
22 


--- Page 23 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF 
COMPENSATION/BENEFITS PANEL 
 
-23- 
Benefit Index is a premier tool for comparing the 
1 
relative worth of one company’s benefits programs to 
2 
those offered by a group of other companies.  It has 
3 
been used by companies since the 1970’s to make such 
4 
assessments. 
5 
Q. 
How are the benefit design competitiveness assessments 
6 
made? 
7 
A. 
Benefit Index results are reached using a very 
8 
specific process.  Actuarial techniques measure the 
9 
total value a representative population of employees 
10 
would derive from O&R’s benefits program and the 
11 
benefits programs of each of the peer companies.  All 
12 
retirement income, death, disability, health care, and 
13 
paid time-off benefits offered to new hire employees 
14 
are included, such as vacation and paid holidays.  
15 
This actuarial analysis reflects the benefits that 
16 
each program would be expected to pay during a year or 
17 
the present value of the benefits employees would be 
18 
expected to earn during a year but receive in the 
19 
future.  The same employee population and assumptions 
20 
are used when measuring the values for each of the 
21 
programs.  This standardization verifies that the 
22 


--- Page 24 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF 
COMPENSATION/BENEFITS PANEL 
 
-24- 
differences are attributable to plan designs, not pay 
1 
levels.  The impact of pay level differences is 
2 
assessed in the benefit package value analysis of the 
3 
Review.  Finally, the benefit design features of O&R’s 
4 
new hired benefits program were compared to the 
5 
average for the peer companies’ new hire benefits 
6 
programs to arrive at a relative benefit design result 
7 
reported by the Benefit Index. 
8 
Q. 
What is a Benefit Index benefit design result? 
9 
A. 
A Benefit Index benefit design result of 100.0 would 
10 
be assigned if O&R’s benefits exactly equaled the 
11 
average of the benefits package value offered by the 
12 
peer companies.  Generally, differences in the overall 
13 
benefit package value are not considered significant 
14 
or material until they exceed ten percent (i.e., less 
15 
than 90.0 or greater than 110.0 as compared to O&R).  
16 
A Benefit Index benefit design result within this 
17 
range would be viewed as “competitive.” 
18 
Q. 
Which benefits programs are included? 
19 
A. 
The benefits analyzed included the following programs 
20 
to which an annualized value was attributed: 
21 


--- Page 25 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF 
COMPENSATION/BENEFITS PANEL 
 
-25- 
• All Post-Retirement Benefits:  Post-retirement 
1 
benefits reviewed included pension, thrift savings 
2 
(401(k) and defined contribution pension plan), 
3 
retiree health, hospital, medical, vision care, 
4 
prescription drug, and life insurance. 
5 
• All Pre-Retirement Benefits:  Pre-retirement 
6 
benefits reviewed included hospital, medical, 
7 
dental, hearing, and vision, and sick, short- and 
8 
long-term disability, cash-based benefits, and paid 
9 
vacation and holidays.

Q. 
How did Aon combine the Benefit Index results with the 
19 
compensation benchmarking to develop the total 
20 
benefits and compensation package value? 
21 
A. 
Aon followed a standard methodology consistent with 
22 


--- Page 27 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF 
COMPENSATION/BENEFITS PANEL 
 
-27- 
industry practice and that Aon employed in the last 
1 
O&R electric and gas base rate cases.  First, Aon 
2 
determined which positions at O&R matched positions 
3 
among the Blended Peer Group, based on a comparison of 
4 
functional responsibilities, job duties, and 
5 
organizational level for which data is available from 
6 
the survey sources.  Next, Aon compared the benefit 
7 
and compensation data for each of these positions at 
8 
O&R to the benefit and compensation data for the same 
9 
positions among the Blended Peer Group companies.  
10 
Finally, Aon aggregated these results to evaluate 
11 
O&R’s overall competitive position relative to the 
12 
Blended Peer Group median.    
13 
Q. 
Why did Aon compare O&R total benefits and 
14 
compensation to the median, but compared the O&R 
15 
benefit designs to the average for the Benefit Index? 
16 
A. 
Median and average are both reasonable methods to make 
17 
observations in a data analysis, and either may be 
18 
used when doing a total benefits and compensation 
19 
analysis.  However, the use of median is an industry 
20 
practice in total benefits and compensation studies 
21 
because the median normalizes a data sample by placing 
22 


--- Page 28 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF 
COMPENSATION/BENEFITS PANEL 
 
-28- 
equal emphasis on each observation, thereby mitigating 
1 
the influence of extreme outlier values, if any.  In 
2 
benefit design reviews, the need to mitigate for 
3 
extreme outliers is less important (program designs, 
4 
not pay levels, are being examined).  Therefore, it is 
5 
a standard industry practice to use market average or 
6 
market typical design when analyzing program design 
7 
features. 
8 
Q. 
If the analysis were based on the average instead of 
9 
the median in the total benefits and compensation 
10 
study, would the result have been materially 
11 
different? 
12 
A. 
No.  The Blended Peer Group results are substantially 
13 
similar using both market reference points.  Using the 
14 
median, O&R’s total benefits and compensation was 2.5 
15 
percent above the Blended Peer Group median (or 102.5 
16 
percent of the median).  Using the average, O&R total 
17 
benefits and compensation was 1.1 percent above the 
18 
Blended Peer Group average (or 101.1 percent of the 
19 
average).

8 
   NON-OFFICER COMPENSATION 
9 
Q. 
Please describe the Company’s overall compensation 
10 
philosophy. 
11 
A. 
The philosophy of the Company is to provide 
12 
compensation that is competitive with the median 
13 
levels of compensation provided by a peer group of 
14 
similarly situated companies.  This approach to 
15 
setting compensation levels permits the Company to be 
16 
reasonably competitive in the labor market and to be 
17 
able to attract, and fairly compensate, employees 
18 
important to the success of the Company.  In targeting 
19 
the median levels for compensation measured against a 
20 
market competitive norm, the Company has taken a 
21 


--- Page 38 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF 
COMPENSATION/BENEFITS PANEL 
 
-38- 
conservative, low-cost approach, which benefits its 
1 
customers. 
2 
Q. 
Does the base compensation for O&R’s non-officer 
3 
management employees include base salary, variable pay 
4 
component, and a long-term equity grant component? 
5 
A. 
Yes.   
6 
Q. 
Is O&R unusual in its inclusion of a variable pay 
7 
component as part of base compensation? 
8 
A. 
No.  Tying a portion of employees’ base compensation 
9 
to performance has become commonplace both in American 
10 
business generally and for public utilities as well.  
11 
Q. 
Please continue. 
12 
A. 
The variable pay component of base compensation in the 
13 
Company’s plan is earned only if the Company reaches 
14 
pre-set financial and operating performance goals that 
15 
are directly linked to specific measurable standards 
16 
consistent with the Company’s goal of providing safe 
17 
and reliable service to customers.  These performance 
18 
goals encompass reliability, safety, customer-service 
19 
performance indicators, environmental excellence, 
20 
public safety and effective cost management.  The 
21 
specific performance goals are tracked on a calendar 
22 


--- Page 39 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF 
COMPENSATION/BENEFITS PANEL 
 
-39- 
year basis and must be achieved each year. 
1 
Q. 
Has the Commission addressed its standards for 
2 
recovery of the variable component of management pay? 
3 
A. 
Yes, the Commission has addressed this topic in 
4 
several O&R rate case related orders.  In its Order 
5 
Denying Petitions for Rehearing and/or Clarification 
6 
issued on November 21, 2011, in Case 10-E-0362 (p. 6) 
7 
the Commission stated: 
8 
The second point we wanted to emphasize is 
9 
that it is not necessary to maintain an 
10 
artificial distinction between compensation in 
11 
the form of traditional pay and benefits and 
12 
compensation that is incentive based. As we 
13 
have stated previously, we recognize that 
14 
variable compensation and incentive plans are 
15 
common management tools aimed at encouraging 
16 
performance improvements that can lead to more 
17 
competitive operations. Consequently, if a 
18 
utility can demonstrate that total 
19 
compensation including incentive compensation 
20 
for a class of employees is reasonable, with a 
21 
comparable total compensation study of 
22 
similarly situated companies being the 
23 
preferred methodology, our concern about the 
24 
relationship of incentive plan objectives to 
25 
ratepayer interests is substantially 
26 
diminished. As long as the plan does not 
27 
promote employee behavior that would be 
28 
contrary to ratepayer interests or Commission 
29 
policies, the fact that it may contain 
30 
financial, budgetary or other goals that 
31 
benefit shareholders as well as ratepayers 
32 
will not, by itself, be grounds for 
33 
disallowing funding in rates, even if the 
34 
relative benefits are unquantified.

Q. 
Please describe the O&R ATIP. 
1 
A. 
ATIP is the variable pay component of non-officer 
2 
management compensation.  The ATIP performance 
3 
measures are approved by the O&R Board at the start of 
4 
the performance period.  At the conclusion of the 
5 
performance period, actual results are compared to the 
6 
established targets and final results are approved by 
7 
the Board.  ATIP represents the portion of employees’ 
8 
annual base salary that is dependent upon the 
9 
attainment of certain predetermined, measurable 
10 
corporate and individual goals.  ATIP must be earned 
11 
each year.  In linking a portion of annual salary to 
12 
defined and measurable performance criteria, the 
13 
Company’s compensation philosophy strives to reward 
14 
each employee’s contribution to the overall operating, 
15 
customer service performance, and financial strength 
16 
of the Company.   
17 
Q. 
Which employees are eligible for ATIP?  
18 
A. 
ATIP is available to all management employees and 
19 
includes both team and individual components.  The 
20 
team portion of the award comprises 60 percent of the 
21 
total available award and the individual portion of 
22 


--- Page 41 ---

DIRECT TESTIMONY OF 
COMPENSATION/BENEFITS PANEL 
 
-41- 
the award comprises 40 percent.  Each employee’s 
1 
potential individual award is based on the 
2 
individual’s contribution toward the overall corporate 
3 
initiatives and achievement of goals, and on his or 
4 
her position within the non-officer management salary 
5 
bands of O&R.  ATIP goals are established annually and 
6 
include both operating and customer service and 
7 
financial targets.  The O&R Board approves the 
8 
corporate goals, and the corporate award in the first 
9 
quarter following the completion of the plan year.

Q. 
What are the implications of seeking to have 
7

8 
employees from a benchmarking perspective? 
9 
A.

10

11 
than salaried employees.  As a result, companies with 
12 
a strategy to maintain comparable benefits among union 
13 
and management employees will often see higher 
14 
benchmarking results when comparing to the programs 
15 
offered to management employees at other companies, 
16 
some of which do not have union employees.

17

18

Q.

Q. 
What are the benefits of offering a Defined 
10 
Contribution Plan to new hires rather than a Defined 
11 
Benefit Plan?  
12 
A.   Defined Contribution Plans transfer the investment and 
13 
longevity risk associated with retirement benefits 
14 
from the Company to the employee.  Over time, Defined 
15 
Benefit Plans can result in significant balance sheet 
16 
liabilities and costs for the Company and for 
17 
customers.  These liabilities and the associated risks 
18 
can grow to be quite significant.  Transitioning to a 
19 
Defined Contribution Plan for all newly hired 
20 
employees curtails the growth of these pension 
21 
liabilities and produces a more stable cost profile 
22 


--- Page 55 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF 
COMPENSATION/BENEFITS PANEL 
 
-55- 
over time. 
1

2

4

14 
Local 503 and management employees covered under the 
15

16 
lifetime monthly pension payment, retiring employees 
17 
can take a single lump payment of their accrued 
18 
benefit. This addition will help to mitigate the 
19 
Company’s Retirement Plan risks and liability over a 
20 
long-term horizon because the employee has assumed the 
21 
investment risk.  The reduction in obligations due to 
22 


--- Page 56 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF 
COMPENSATION/BENEFITS PANEL 
 
-56- 
offering the lump sum to O&R Local 503 participants is 
1 
estimated to be $1,175,000, based on the census data 
2 
and valuation assumptions used for the calculation of 
3

4 
Q.  Please describe the change to the “Pivot Year.” 
5 
A. 
The Labor Contract provides for a pivot year change 
6 
from January 1, 2014 to January 1, 2016 for employees 
7 
who retire on or after January 1, 2020 and for 
8 
retirements on or after January 1, 2023, the Pivot 
9 
Year will change to January 1, 2019. Pivot year 
10 
changes are a common practice under the CAP pension 
11 
formula.  The Pivot Year element of the CAP pension 
12 
formula provides a snapshot in time that determines 
13 
both the salary and qualifying years of service for 
14 
calculating the various components of the pension 
15 
plan. Specifically, the CAP pension formula is 
16 
comprised of these parts: a prior service accrual 
17 
equal to 1.5 percent of the salary rate as of January 
18 
1 of the Pivot Year multiplied by the years of service 
19 
from the pension plan entry date to the respective 
20 
Pivot Year, and a future service accrual which is 
21 
equal to two percent of base earnings accumulated from 
22 


--- Page 57 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF 
COMPENSATION/BENEFITS PANEL 
 
-57- 
the Pivot Year date to the date of retirement.  The 
1 
formula also provides for an additional future service 
2 
accrual equal to two times the annual salary rate in 
3 
effect upon retirement multiplied by two percent.  The 
4 
total pension level is simply the sum of these parts. 
5 
Q. 
How is the CAP pension formula different from a final 
6 
average salary pension? 
7 
A.  A final average pay formula automatically updates 
8 
earnings, usually based on an average of earnings in 
9 
the last several years of employment, the CAP pension 
10 
formula does not have an automatic method to update 
11 
earnings.  Instead, the Pivot Year updates serve as 
12 
the method to update earnings similar to the way final 
13 
average salary pension formula earnings are updated.  
14 
Q. 
Why is updating the Pivot Year important?  
15 
A. 
The replacement income attributed to a pension benefit 
16 
significantly diminishes if the underlying earnings 
17 
component of the formula is not periodically updated 
18 
by moving the Pivot Year.

Q. 
What drives the cost of health care? 
3

4

5

6

7

9

10 
more procedures, more supplies, and use of different 
11 
technology for the same condition than in 2017 or the 
12 
use of new treatments for previously untreatable 
13 
terminal conditions.   
14 
Q.

15 
health care costs?  
16 
A.  New medical technologies (such as brain implant 
17 
therapies for spinal cord injuries or other 
18 
neurological injuries) raise the cost of medical 
19 
services because they are not designed to compete with 
20 
existing technologies.  Rather, they are designed and 
21 
introduced into the market to enhance the ability of 
22 


--- Page 75 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF 
COMPENSATION/BENEFITS PANEL 
 
-75- 
medical professionals to save the lives of patients, 
1 
something that is difficult to put a price tag on.   
2

3 
medical care?  
4 
A.

5 
expensive diagnostic studies doctors order to protect 
6 
themselves from potential litigation (known as 
7

8

to costs and may contribute up to 34% of the annual 
10 
healthcare costs in the U.S.  
11

15

16 
increased costs to companies providing health care. A 
17 
large portion of the increased spending for 
18 
prescription drugs is attributed to an increase in

7

11

12

9

10

11

12

HEALTHCARE INFLATION 
15 
Q. 
What kind of inflation factor should be used to 
16 
project health care expenses? 
17 
A. 
When predicting future health care costs, we believe 
18 
that the inflation factor supplied by the various 
19 
health insurance carriers in combination with the 
20 
Company’s health care cost experience will result in a 
21 
better estimate.  The inflation factor supplied by 
22 


--- Page 80 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF 
COMPENSATION/BENEFITS PANEL 
 
-80- 
insurance carriers not only includes the effects of 
1 
general inflation on the health care market but also 
2 
incorporates how the other factors described above 
3 
impact future medical inflation.    
4 
Q. 
What is the impact on health care expenses of using a 
5 
general inflation increase for projecting health care 
6 
expenses instead of using a health care projection 
7 
rate which factors in the different health care cost 
8 
drivers? 
9 
A. 
Projecting health care costs using a general inflation 
10 
factor instead of a health care specific projection 
11 
rate that factors in the cost drivers described above 
12 
results in an understatement of health care expenses 
13 
that should be recovered as a reasonable business 
14 
expense.  
15 
Q. 
What has been the Company’s actual healthcare 
16 
inflation experience during the historical year?  
17 
A. 
For the first time in this historical year, the health 
18 
care cost trend is in alignment with general inflation 
19 
but this trend is during the period of significant 
20 
disruption in medical services and providers due to 
21 
COVID-19. 
22 


--- Page 81 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF 
COMPENSATION/BENEFITS PANEL 
 
-81- 
 Q. What has been the Company’s history of health care 
1 
inflation when compared to a general inflation measure 
2 
such as GDP?  
3 
A. 
A comparison of the last three years actual growth in 
4 
health care expenses to an increase solely based on 
5 
general inflation in each of those years results in an 
6 
understatement of actual care costs for the three 
7 
years of over $1 million.   
8 
Q. 
What are the impacts on this filing if a GDP factor 
9 
were used to measure healthcare inflation?  
10 
A. 
The imposition of the GDP factor for the escalation of 
11 
health care costs instead of the expected health care 
12 
trend factor included in this filing would result in 
13 
an understatement of health care costs for the three 
14 
rate years of over $2 million. 
15 
Q. 
Why is the Consumer Price Index (“CPI”) an 
16 
inappropriate measure for health care inflation?  
17 
A. 
A general inflation factor, such as the CPI, based on 
18 
the cost of goods, services, and labor that affect all 
19 
sectors of the economy, measures the average price 
20 
change over time for a constant-quality, constant-
21 
quantity market basket of goods and services but fails 
22 


--- Page 82 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF 
COMPENSATION/BENEFITS PANEL 
 
-82- 
to include the changes in the size and age structure 
1 
of the population that affect the number of people 
2 
using health care services.   
3 
Q. 
Please continue. 
4 
A. 
A general inflation factor may capture medical price 
5 
inflation, i.e., increases in the cost of providing a 
6 
unit of care above and beyond inflation in the general 
7 
economy, but not the increase attributed to the type 
8 
of care, technology used, and services per unit of 
9 
care delivered.     
10

11

12

13

14

15 
503 participants in a two percent contribution and to 
16 
escalate the contributions by one percent per year to 
17 
ten percent of pay.  Effective January 1, 2021 newly 
18 
hired management employees will automatically be 
19 
enrolled in the Thrift Savings Plan with a three 
20 
percent contribution and the contributions will be 
21 
escalated by 1.0 percent per year up to 15.0 percent 
22

of pay. This is intended to assist the employees in 
1 
accumulating sufficient assets for retirement.   
2 
Q. 
Were any changes made to the Employer Match program? 
3 
A. 
Yes. Effective January 1, 2020 the Company provides an 
4 
Employer Match of 100 percent of the first 4 percent 
5 
of base salary contributed to the Plan for union 
6 
employees covered by the cash balance formula or 
7 
defined contribution plan.  
8 
Q. 
Were any other changes made to the Thrift Savings Plan 
9 
as a result of the Local 503 union contract? 
10 
A. 
Yes. Effective January 1, 2020, Local 503 employees 
11 
may contribute to a Roth 401(k) which is an after-tax 
12 
option.  Having both a Roth and a pre-tax 401(k) plan 
13 
for retirement can help employees balance their 
14 
retirement income.

POST EMPLOYMENT BENEFITS OTHER THAN PENSIONS (“OPEBS”) 
17 
Q.

18 
A. 
The Company’s OPEB programs are comprised of the 
19 
Retiree Health Program, which includes major medical, 
20 
hospitalization, vision, and pharmaceutical benefits.  
21 
The Company also offers a limited retiree term life 
22

-84- 
insurance program. 
1 
Q. 
What is the status of the Company’s OPEB plans? 
2 
A. 
Starting with the Retiree Health Program, O&R offers 
3 
management retirees who are age 55 with ten years of 
4 
service at the time they retire from employment, and 
5 
their eligible dependents, a voluntary Retiree Health 
6 
Program.  The Retiree Health Program offers enrolled 
7 
retirees a prescription drug plan and comprehensive 
8 
hospital, medical, and vision care plans with a 
9 
network of participating providers.  Once a retiree or 
10 
covered dependent becomes eligible for Medicare, the 
11 
Retiree Health Program coordinates his or her health 
12 
care expenses with Medicare.  For Medicare-eligible 
13 
retirees, Medicare is the primary payer of hospital 
14 
and medical claims, and the Retiree Health Program is 
15 
the secondary payer.  Under the prescription drug 
16 
plan, once a retiree and covered dependent become 
17 
eligible for Medicare Part D, retirees may continue 
18 
their coverage under the Retiree Health Program or 
19 
enroll in the Medicare program for their prescription 
20 
drug coverage.  The Company also provides retired 
21 
management employees who were age 50 as of January 1, 
22 


--- Page 85 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF 
COMPENSATION/BENEFITS PANEL 
 
-85- 
2013, with retiree term life insurance benefits of 
1 
$25,000. Local 503 retirees are eligible for $12,500 
2 
in life insurance at no cost if they retire at age 55 
3 
with 10 years of service.  
4 
Q. 
Does the Labor Contract provide retiree health benefit 
5

7

11

12 
A. 
For the Retiree Health Program, the Company 
13

14 
management employees retiring under the CAP pension 
15 
formula.  Under the cost-sharing formula, the 
16 
Company’s contribution toward program costs is limited 
17 
to its contribution in the preceding year plus 
18 
inflation as measured by the change in the CPI.  
19 
Contributions for retirees increase if Retiree Health 
20 
Program cost increases are above CPI. 
21 
Q. 
How has the cost sharing changed the cost of coverage

IS and Cyber Security
Career (P3)
● Evaluates, tests, recommends, develops, coordinates, monitors and maintains informaƟon systems (IS) and cyber security 
policies, procedures and systems, including access management for hardware, firmware and software
● Ensures that IS and cyber security architecture/designs, plans, controls, processes, standards, policies and procedures are 
aligned with IS standards and overall IS and cyber security
● IdenƟﬁes security risks and exposures, determines the causes of security violaƟons and suggests procedures to halt 
future incidents and improve security
● Develops techniques and procedures for conducƟng IS and cyber security risk assessments and compliance audits, the 
evaluation and testing of hardware, firmware and software for possible impact on system security, and the investigation 
and resolution of security incidents
● Implements IS and cyber security policies and takes measures against intrusion, frauds, aƩacks or leaks

policies, procedures and systems, including access management for hardware, firmware and software
● Ensures that IS and cyber security architecture/designs, plans, controls, processes, standards, policies and procedures are 
aligned with IS standards and overall IS and cyber security
● IdenƟﬁes security risks and exposures, determines the causes of security violaƟons and suggests procedures to halt 
future incidents and improve security
● Develops techniques and procedures for conducƟng IS and cyber security risk assessments and compliance audits, the 
evaluation and testing of hardware, firmware and software for possible impact on system security, and the investigation 
and resolution of security incidents
● Implements IS and cyber security policies and takes measures against intrusion, frauds, aƩacks or leaks

Energy 
Efficiency/Conservation 
Generalist/Multidiscipline
Manager (M2)
● Develops, promotes, and implements energy eﬃciency (EE), demand side management (DSM), demand response (DR) 
and conservation programs to meet established goals
● Develops, promotes and implements EE programs for new construcƟon and upgrades to exisƟng buildings such as Ɵghter 
building envelopes (e.g., insulation) and HVAC ducts, high-efficiency HVAC equipment and HVAC tune-ups to reduce energy 
demand and meet regulatory and corporate goals
● Develops, promotes and implements DSM programs such as energy surveys, weatherizaƟon, load management, eﬃcient 
lighting retrofits and tighter building envelopes
● Develops, promotes and implements DR programs such as load management, standby generaƟon, pricing signals and 
interruptible service
● Provides customers with technical advice and assistance to design, procure and install energy eﬃcient equipment and 
adopt demand-reduction initiatives
● Conducts cost-beneﬁt analyses and evaluates the impact of EE, DSM and DR programs
● Ensures that programs are in compliance with regulatory guidelines and may be responsible for Public UƟlity Commission 
(PUC) funds awarded for DSM/EE programs

● Ensures compliance with all safety, training and auditable guidelines, policies, pracƟces, organizaƟon standards and

● Matching note: Match Group Managers (Career Level M4) who may have the Ɵtle of district manager within a division and 
typically report to the Regional/Division Executive (CDB Energy Services Executive Compensation Survey benchmark EED000-
EX) to this Discipline. These district managers are typically responsible for construction, operation, maintenance and field

● Has responsibiliƟes that typically include operaƟons, line construcƟon and/or maintenance and ﬁeld engineering
● Plans, directs and coordinates acƟviƟes to prevent electric service interrupƟons and minimize the impact and restoraƟon 
time of service interruptions
● Schedules maintenance, trouble operaƟons/service restoraƟon, metering operaƟons and operaƟons planning work
● Provides adequate, accurate informaƟon to the customer service centers to keep aﬀected customers properly informed
● Ensures compliance with all safety, training and auditable guidelines, policies, pracƟces, organizaƟon standards and 
government regulations
● Matching note: Match Group Managers (Career Level M4) who may have the Ɵtle of district manager within a division and 
typically report to the Regional/Division Executive (CDB Energy Services Executive Compensation Survey benchmark EED000-
EX) to this Discipline. These district managers are typically responsible for construction, operation, maintenance and field 
engineering, customer services, marketing/energy services, and community services.

● Acts promptly to return the system to a stable state when voltage and/or frequency excursions occur
● Maintains real-Ɵme compliance with reliability standards set by regulatory authoriƟes

Energy Management 
System Software 
Development
Specialist (P4)
● Analyzes, researches, develops, maintains and implements enhancements to the energy management system (EMS), the 
supervisory control and data acquisition (SCADA) system and other electric control center automation systems
● Operates and maintains computer and control systems including remote components required for the electric systems 
operation center to monitor, control and optimize electric system performance to reduce energy consumption, improve 
system utilization and increase reliability by using real-time data such as frequency, actual generation and load flows
● Implements and manages the electric system security program that protects the system from threats in accordance with 
North American Electric Reliability Corporation (NERC) cyber security standards
● Provides detailed electrical power grid security analysis informaƟon for ﬁeld test engineers in diagnosing, troubleshooƟng 
and resolving problems with the EMS metering and SCADA subsystems
● Maintains in-depth knowledge of new and emerging technologies required to provide long-term enhancements to the 
reliability, performance and functionality of the EMS and other transmission control center automation systems
● Coordinates with the EMS network model engineers on the development and maintenance of the EMS network models
● Tests EMS network models, SCADA and soŌware changes to ensure that network applicaƟons produce consistent and 
credible results
● Supports the speciﬁcaƟon, design, tesƟng and development of the EMS system to ensure that it meets changing 
functional requirements
● Matching note: Match EMS programmers to this Discipline.

operation center to monitor, control and optimize electric system performance to reduce energy consumption, improve 
system utilization and increase reliability by using real-time data such as frequency, actual generation and load flows
● Implements and manages the electric system security program that protects the system from threats in accordance with 
North American Electric Reliability Corporation (NERC) cyber security standards
● Provides detailed electrical power grid security analysis informaƟon for ﬁeld test engineers in diagnosing, troubleshooƟng 
and resolving problems with the EMS metering and SCADA subsystems
● Maintains in-depth knowledge of new and emerging technologies required to provide long-term enhancements to the 
reliability, performance and functionality of the EMS and other transmission control center automation systems
● Coordinates with the EMS network model engineers on the development and maintenance of the EMS network models
● Tests EMS network models, SCADA and soŌware changes to ensure that network applicaƟons produce consistent and 
credible results
● Supports the speciﬁcaƟon, design, tesƟng and development of the EMS system to ensure that it meets changing 
functional requirements
● Matching note: Match EMS programmers to this Discipline.

Energy Delivery/Distribution 
Generalist/Multidiscipline
Career (P3)
● Directs and coordinates distribuƟon acƟviƟes that provide electric and/or gas service to customers
● Has responsibiliƟes that typically include operaƟons, line construcƟon and/or maintenance and ﬁeld engineering
● Plans, directs and coordinates acƟviƟes to prevent electric service interrupƟons and minimize the impact and restoraƟon 
time of service interruptions
● Schedules maintenance, trouble operaƟons/service restoraƟon, metering operaƟons and operaƟons planning work
● Provides adequate, accurate informaƟon to the customer service centers to keep aﬀected customers properly informed
● Ensures compliance with all safety, training and auditable guidelines, policies, pracƟces, organizaƟon standards and 
government regulations
● Matching note: Match Group Managers (Career Level M4) who may have the Ɵtle of district manager within a division and 
typically report to the Regional/Division Executive (CDB Energy Services Executive Compensation Survey benchmark EED000-
EX) to this Discipline. These district managers are typically responsible for construction, operation, maintenance and field 
engineering, customer services, marketing/energy services, and community services.

• Coordinates interface among various stakeholders (e.g., procurement, health and safety, quality control,engineering, 
construction, project management)
• Documents commissioning procedures, provides operational assistance and troubleshooting and conducts training
